At night, I often wake up sweating from these half-remembered dreams about the accident and just can't get back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain people, places, or activities that remind you of the accident where you got shocked at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I absolutely do. I've been making up excuses to stay in the office doing paperwork or sending the younger apprentices to open up panels instead of doing it myself. The mere thought of standing in front of live gear again makes my stomach completely flip, so I just can't bring myself to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in safe environments, like at home or in public places?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I constantly feel wired and jumpy, like I'm just waiting for something terrible to happen. It doesn't even matter if I'm in a safe place, like sitting on the couch with my wife or trying to watch a ball game—I just can't shake that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how long you've been experiencing these feelings of dread, uneasiness, and jumpiness since the accident, and have they been consistent or have they changed over time?\n\nPatient: The accident itself was about six months ago, but this dread and uneasiness really started building up over the last four months. Since then, it's been completely constant and hasn't let up at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to feel emotions or connect with others since the accident, such as feeling detached from your loved ones or lacking interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my connections with others have definitely suffered. By the time I get home, I'm so exhausted and on edge that I just zone out with some beers instead of really talking to my wife, and my social life has pretty much slipped away because I don't do anything with my friends anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for what happened during the accident at work, or do you think that if you had done something differently, the outcome might have been better?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I constantly blame myself and can't shake the thought that I'm a screw-up who should have known better. I feel like since I messed up that badly once, it's only a matter of time before I make another mistake and kill myself or someone else on the job.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, or like things around you aren't real, especially when you're having those flashbacks or feeling really anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel like I'm outside of my body or that things aren't real. It's more of an intense feeling of panic and physical tension, like my chest tightening up, rather than feeling disconnected like that."

The mere thought of standing in front of live gear again makes my stomach completely flip, so I just can't bring myself to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in safe environments, like at home or in public places?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I constantly feel wired and jumpy, like I'm just waiting for something terrible to happen. It doesn't even matter if I'm in a safe place, like sitting on the couch with my wife or trying to watch a ball game—I just can't shake that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how long you've been experiencing these feelings of dread, uneasiness, and jumpiness since the accident, and have they been consistent or have they changed over time?\n\nPatient: The accident itself was about six months ago, but this dread and uneasiness really started building up over the last four months. Since then, it's been completely constant and hasn't let up at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to feel emotions or connect with others since the accident, such as feeling detached from your loved ones or lacking interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my connections with others have definitely suffered. By the time I get home, I'm so exhausted and on edge that I just zone out with some beers instead of really talking to my wife, and my social life has pretty much slipped away because I don't do anything with my friends anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for what happened during the accident at work, or do you think that if you had done something differently, the outcome might have been better?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I constantly blame myself and can't shake the thought that I'm a screw-up who should have known better. I feel like since I messed up that badly once, it's only a matter of time before I make another mistake and kill myself or someone else on the job.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, or like things around you aren't real, especially when you're having those flashbacks or feeling really anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel like I'm outside of my body or that things aren't real. It's more of an intense feeling of panic and physical tension, like my chest tightening up, rather than feeling disconnected like that.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned not feeling like you're outside of your body, but can you tell me if during those flashbacks or when you're feeling really anxious, you've ever felt like you're in a daze or spaced out, as if you're not fully present in what's happening around you?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel spaced out or in a daze at all. It's actually the opposite—I feel incredibly present and completely overwhelmed by the anxiety and the memory of it. It's like I'm highly alert to every single detail of the panic in those moments."
  }

Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.
